Uniform spanning trees and random matrix statistics
Nathanaël Berestycki, Marcin Lis, Mingchang Liu, and Eveliina Peltola

Abstract
We consider a uniform spanning tree in a δ \delta -square grid approximation of a planar domain Ω \Omega . For given integer n ≥ 2 n\geq 2 , we condition the tree on the following n n -arm event: we pick n n branches, emanating from n n points microscopically close to a given interior point, and condition them to connect to the boundary ∂ Ω \partial\Omega without intersecting. What can be said about the geometry of these branches? We derive an exact formula for the characteristic function of the total winding of the branches, involving the Brownian loop measure in the scaling limit δ → 0 \delta\to 0 . A surprising consequence of this formula is that in the scaling limit, the behaviour of this function depends on the total number of branches n n only through its parity. We also describe the scaling limit of the branches. If Ω = 𝔻 \Omega=\mathbb{D} is the unit disc, then they hit the boundary of 𝔻 \mathbb{D} (i.e., the unit circle) at random positions which coincide exactly with the eigenvalues of a random matrix of size n n drawn from the Circular Orthogonal Ensemble (COE, also called C β \beta E with β = 1 \beta=1 ). Furthermore, the branches converge to Loewner evolution driven by the circular Dyson Brownian motion with parameter β = 4 \beta=4 (i.e., n n -sided radial SLE κ \SLE_{\kappa} with κ = 2 \kappa=2 ). We thus verify a prediction made by Cardy in this setting. Along the way, we develop a flow-line (imaginary geometry) coupling of n n -sided radial SLE κ \SLE_{\kappa} with the Gaussian free field, which may be of independent interest. Surprisingly, we find that the variance of the corresponding field near the singularity also does not depend on the number n ≥ 2 n\geq 2 of curves. In contrast, the variance of the the winding of the curves behaves as κ / n 2 \kappa/n^{2} , which agrees with the predictions from the physics literature made by Wieland and Wilson numerically, and by Duplantier and Binder using Coulomb gas methods — but disagrees with a result of Kenyon.



UST
on a
40\times 40
square, conditioned so that four given neighbouring branches do not intersect.

1
Introduction and main results

It is well known that the scaling limit of a
uniform spanning tree
(UST) in two dimensions
almost surely contains exceptional
triple points
(points which, if removed, produce three connected components) but no points of higher multiplicity
[
Ben99
]
.
In this article, we consider the question of conditioning a uniform spanning tree on the event that a given interior point, say
z\in\Omega
, has a given arbitrary (fixed) multiplicity,
n\geq 2
.
This is a degenerate conditioning if
n\geq 3
, which needs to be carefully defined.
The main goal of this work is to answer the following question: what can be said about the geometry of these curves?
For ease, we will consider USTs on finite subgraphs of the square lattice
\delta\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with mesh size
\delta>0
, for which the conditioning can be defined relatively simply (see below for precise definitions).

We find two types of results.
On the one hand, we obtain an exact formula, valid at the lattice level (for fixed
\delta>0
), for the characteristic function of the
total winding
(i.e., algebraic total number of crossings by the
n
branches of a fixed reference line); see Theorem
1.1
.
This formula can be analysed in the scaling limit where the mesh size

\delta
tends to zero, in terms of a certain Brownian loop measure.
A surprising consequence of our formula is that the total winding has a limit law which is independent of
n
.

On the other hand, we fully describe the scaling limit of the law of these
n
curves (with
n
fixed, while
\delta\to 0
),
and reveal a connection to objects appearing naturally in the context of
random matrix theory

(specifically, the Circular
\beta
-Ensemble, C
\beta
E with
\beta=1
and
\beta=4
),
which correspond to predictions originally made by Cardy
[
Car03
]
;
see Theorem
1.2
.
The former describes the density of the hitting points of the curves to the outer boundary, while the latter describes the the Loewner evolution of the curves themselves (as a variant of the Schramm–Loewner evolution process, SLE, with
\kappa=2
).
In addition, we describe the topological winding of the curves, generalising Schramm’s result for a single radial SLE;
see Proposition
1.4
.
We also derive a coupling of these curves with a variant of the Gaussian free field,
which fit into the imaginary geometry framework à la Miller & Sheffield; see Theorem
1.4
.

1.1
Statement of the problem

Let us first define more precisely the conditioning that we have in mind.
Suppose that
\Omega\subsetneq\mathbb{C}
is a doubly connected domain, i.e.,

\Omega
is conformally equivalent to an annulus, so
\partial\Omega
has two connected components.
Denote by
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega
the boundary of the unbounded connected component of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Omega
and define
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega:=\partial\Omega\setminus\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega
.
We will suppose that
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega
is analytic and
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega
is at least a Jordan curve; the inside of this Jordan curve (i.e., the unique bounded component of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega)
is a simply connected domain which we denote by
\Omega_{\scalebox{0.6}{{fill}}}
.
We may assume without loss of generality that
0
is separated from
\infty
by
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega
.

Suppose
\{\Omega^{\delta}=(\mathcal{V}^{\delta},\mathcal{E}^{\delta})\}_{\delta>0}
is a sequence of finite graphs such that

\Omega^{\delta}\subset\delta\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
\Omega^{\delta}
is doubly connected for every
\delta>0
.
Define
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
and
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega^{\delta}
similarly as above.
Using the usual planar self-duality of the square lattice, each vertex
v\in\mathcal{V}^{\delta}
can be identified with a square
f(v)
of sidelength
\delta
centred at
v
.
To the graph
\Omega^{\delta}
, we can then associate an open set
\tilde{\Omega}^{\delta}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\approx\mathbb{C}
given by
\tilde{\Omega}^{\delta}:=\textnormal{Int}(\smash{\underset{v\in\mathcal{V}^{\delta}}{\cup}}\,\overline{f(v)})
.

Setup.

In the scaling limit results, we assume that
\Omega^{\delta}
converges to
\Omega
as
\delta\to 0
in the following sense:

1.

There exists a conformal isomorphism
\phi=\phi_{r}
from
\Omega=\Omega_{r}
with some
r=r_{\Omega}\in(0,1)

onto an annulus
\mathbb{A}_{r}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\,|\,r<|z|<1\}

and a sequence
(\phi^{\delta})_{\delta>0}
of conformal isomorphisms from
\tilde{\Omega}^{\delta}

onto
\mathbb{A}_{r^{\delta}}
such that
r^{\delta}\to r
as
\delta\to 0
and
(\phi^{\delta})^{-1}
converges to
\phi^{-1}
locally uniformly on
\mathbb{A}_{r}
as
\delta\to 0
.

2.

Using the conventional metric
\dist_{X}(\cdot,\,\cdot)
of unparametrised curves defined in Equation (
3.19
) (which is the metric of uniform convergence up to reparametrisation),
we have
\dist_{X}(\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta},\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega)\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
,
and there exists a constant
C=C_{\Omega}>0
such that
\dist_{X}(\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega^{\delta},\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega)\leq C\delta
for every
\delta>0
.

Figure 1.1
:
Uniform spanning tree on the graph
\Omega^{\delta}
(gray), with wired (resp. free) boundary conditions on
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
(resp.
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega^{\delta}
), conditioned on the event
E^{\delta}_{\bm{x}}
, case
n=2
.
The components containing the special points
x_{1}^{\delta}
and
x_{2}^{\delta}
are in blue. Other components are in red.
All components of the tree are oriented towards the wired boundary
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
.
The two disjoint oriented paths
\gamma_{1}^{\delta}
and
\gamma_{2}^{\delta}
, from
x_{1}^{\delta}
and
x_{2}^{\delta}
respectively to
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
, have been highlighted in dashed blue.
In the right panel, we superimpose the corresponding dual spanning tree on the planar dual lattice (in green). Its components (three in this example) are naturally oriented towards the inner boundary.

We fix
n
distinct vertices
x_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,x_{n}^{\delta}
on the inner boundary
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega^{\delta}
, by convention labelled in counterclockwise order,
and assume that each
x^{\delta}_{j}
converges to
x_{j}\in\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega
, where
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are
n
distinct points on
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega
.
We will consider the uniform spanning tree
\mathcal{T}^{\delta}
on
\Omega^{\delta}
with
wired
1
1

1

For simplicity, we will not distinguish the graph
\Omega^{\delta}
and the graph obtained from
\Omega^{\delta}
by wiring the outer boundary
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
to a root vertex
\varrho^{\delta}
. We assume these boundary conditions throughout.

boundary condition on
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
and free boundary condition on
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega^{\delta}
,
i.e., we view the outer boundary
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
as a single vertex
\varrho^{\delta}
declared to be the root of the UST.
Observe that
\mathcal{T}^{\delta}
can be viewed as an
arborescence
, i.e., an acyclic collection of oriented edges in
\Omega^{\delta}
such that every vertex
x\in\Omega^{\delta}
distinct from the root has a unique outgoing edge.
For
1\leq j\leq n
, define
\gamma^{\delta}_{j}=\vec{\gamma}^{\delta}_{j}
to be the
UST branch
in
\mathcal{T}^{\delta}
connecting
x_{j}^{\delta}
to the root vertex
\varrho^{\delta}
, obtained by following the outgoing edges emanating from
x_{j}^{\delta}
inductively, and which necessarily terminates at the root
\varrho^{\delta}
.
We primarily view
\gamma_{j}^{\delta}
as an unordered collection of vertices,
\gamma_{j}^{\delta}=\{x_{j}^{\delta},\ldots,\varrho^{\delta}\}
, though sometimes it will be useful to identify it with the ordered collection
\vec{\gamma}^{\delta}_{j}
of edges it successively traverses, and sometimes as a continuous curve up to reparametrisation ending at
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
.
With slight abuse of notation, we usually denote the path as
\gamma_{j}^{\delta}
; we highlight the orientation as
\vec{\gamma}^{\delta}_{j}
and its reverse as
\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\gamma$}}$}{}^{\delta}_{j}
only when necessary.

Let us now define the event we are interested in conditioning on — see Figure
1.1
for an illustration.
For
\bm{x}^{\delta}=(x_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,x_{n}^{\delta})
as above, we define

\displaystyle E^{\delta}_{\bm{x}}=E(\Omega^{\delta};x_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,x_{n}^{\delta}):=\big\{\mathcal{T}^{\delta}\colon\gamma^{\delta}_{i}\cap\gamma^{\delta}_{j}=\{\varrho^{\delta}\}\textnormal{ for all }1\leq i<j\leq n\big\},

(1.1)

which is the event that the branches emanating from
x_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,x_{n}^{\delta}
are pairwise disjoint; i.e.,
\gamma^{\delta}_{i}
and
\gamma^{\delta}_{j}
for
i\neq j
do not have any common vertex except for the root vertex
\varrho^{\delta}
.
Events of this type feature prominently in the theory of SLEs, since they determine the crossing and/or arm exponents, whose computation was one of the major motivation and outstanding success of the theory
[
LSW01a
,
LSW01b
,
LSW02
]
.
See also
[
Ken00b
]
and the physics
literature
[
DB02
,
WW03
,
Dup06
]

(to which we will come back later in Remark
1.7
)
more specifically for computations precisely in this case of the uniform spanning tree.

In the present work, we are interested in letting the mesh size
\delta
tend to zero and in letting the inner boundary
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega
shrink to a point, meaning that
r\to 0
.
This can be done simultaneously or in different orders, and either way we do not expect this choice to impact the answers (we will partly justify this with our results).
For technical reasons, it will turn out to be more convenient to first let the mesh size
\delta\to 0
(keeping
\Omega
and in particular
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega
fixed) and subsequently letting
\diam(\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega)\to 0
;
though some of our results (in particular concerning the winding of the curves conditional on the event
E^{\delta}_{\bm{x}}
) will also be valid independently of the order in which these limits are taken.

1.2
Total winding

A question which is particularly natural concerns the winding of the UST branches near their common endpoint.
To address this, we begin with some generalities about different notions of winding for curves, and an intuitive heuristics that guides the subsequent results.
If
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{C}
is a smooth simple curve with
\gamma^{\prime}(t)\neq 0
for
t\in[a,b]
, the (intrinsic) winding of
\gamma
is, by definition, the quantity
\int_{a}^{b}\arg\gamma^{\prime}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t
.
This quantity extends
2
2

2

Note that the intrinsic winding is ill-defined for fractal paths, for which we use the topological winding instead.
They can be related in the piecewise smooth case; see
[
BLR20
, Section 2]
.

without any difficulty to piecewise smooth paths such as the lattice paths of interest,
\bm{\gamma}^{\delta}=(\gamma_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,\gamma_{n}^{\delta})
.
In this case, the winding of such a path also coincides with the sum of turning angles.

Heuristics.

Fix
k\geq 1
, and suppose
r\ll\mathrm{e}^{-k}
. We consider the
n
branches between scales
\mathrm{e}^{-k}
and
\mathrm{e}^{-k-1}
, and more specifically, the winding of these branches at this scale,
say in the sense of the algebraic number of full turns around the annulus.
For topological reasons, as the curves do not intersect, each branch accumulates the same winding between these two scales.
Also, scale-invariance suggests that the law of the winding accumulated between scales
\mathrm{e}^{-k}
and
\mathrm{e}^{-k-1}
does not depend on
k
, and it seems reasonable to guess that these increments are also roughly independent as
k
varies.
It is therefore natural to expect that the winding up to scale
\mathrm{e}^{-k}
evolves like a Brownian motion as a function of the logarithm
k
of the scale, but with a variance that needs to be determined
— and should in particular depend in an interesting way on the number
n\geq 2
of branches.
Intuitively, the larger
n
is, the more rigid is the system, and thus the smaller we should expect the variance to be.
We will see that
the variance behaves quadratically in
n
:

\displaystyle\textnormal{variance of winding}\sim\frac{2}{n^{2}},

(1.2)

up to any given scale, for each curve.
(Here, the constant
2=\kappa
is the variance of the corresponding
\SLE_{\kappa}
driving function, also discussed below. In general, the variance of the winding turns out to be
\sim\kappa/n^{2}
.)

An exact law.

To state our exact result concerning total winding, it is convenient to consider a slightly more restricted event where we impose the locations at which
the branches
\bm{\gamma}^{\delta}=(\gamma_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,\gamma_{n}^{\delta})
hit the outer boundary.
Let
v_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,v_{n}^{\delta}
be
n
distinct vertices on
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
, by convention labelled in counterclockwise order.
We use “
\cdot\longleftrightarrow\cdot
” to indicate that there is a connection in the tree
\mathcal{T}^{\delta}
.
Thus, bearing in mind that
\mathcal{T}^{\delta}
is an arborescence oriented towards
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
,
the event
\{x_{i}^{\delta}\longleftrightarrow v_{j}^{\delta}\}
means that, following successively the unique oriented branch
\vec{\gamma}_{i}^{\delta}
started at
x_{i}^{\delta}
, one eventually arrives at the vertex
v_{j}^{\delta}
at time
\tau_{j}^{\delta}
.

Using the convention that
v_{j}^{\delta}=v_{j-n}^{\delta}
for
j>n
, we now define

\displaystyle E^{\delta}_{\bm{x},\bm{v},k}:=\;

\displaystyle\big\{x_{1}^{\delta}\longleftrightarrow v_{k+1}^{\delta},\,\ldots,\,x_{n}^{\delta}\longleftrightarrow v_{n+k}^{\delta}\big\}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\big\{\mathcal{T}^{\delta}\;|\;\vec{\gamma}^{\delta}_{1}(\tau_{1}^{\delta})=v_{k+1}^{\delta},\,\ldots,\,\vec{\gamma}_{n}(\tau_{n}^{\delta})=v_{k+n}^{\delta}\big\},\qquad\textnormal{for }0\leq k\leq n-1,

(1.3)

\displaystyle E^{\delta}_{\bm{x},\bm{v}}=\;

\displaystyle E(\Omega^{\delta};x_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,x_{n}^{\delta};v_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,v_{n}^{\delta}):=\bigcup_{k=0}^{n-1}E^{\delta}_{\bm{x},\bm{v},k}.

(1.4)

Due to planarity, the event
E^{\delta}_{\bm{x},\bm{v}}
is exactly the event that
E^{\delta}_{\bm{x}}
occurs and
\{\{x_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,x_{n}^{\delta}\}\longleftrightarrow\{v_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,v_{n}^{\delta}\}\}
:
indeed, if the latter occurs, then necessarily these connections must respect the counterclockwise order.

Let
K(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})
be the (signed) number of crossings by
\gamma_{j}^{\delta}
across a given line (“zipper”) connecting the two boundary components of the annulus (see Figure
1.2
for an illustration).
Our result, Theorem
1.1
below, gives an exact identity for the law of the
t
otal winding
3
3

3

This crossing number differs from the actual intrinsic winding, normalised by
2\pi
, by at most
\pm 1
; see Equation (
2.9
).

K(\bm{\gamma}^{\delta}):=\sum_{j=1}^{n}2\pi K(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})
.

Our identity takes a different form according to whether
n
is odd or even, but involves either way the (discrete)
random walk loop soup
on
\Omega^{\delta}
(see Section
2.1
and
[
LF04
]
for definitions,
bearing in mind that random walks on
\Omega^{\delta}
are only reflected at
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega^{\delta}
but absorbed at
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
).
Denote by
\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}
the set of (discrete) uncontractible loops on
\Omega^{\delta}
(i.e., loops not homotopic to a point) which do not hit
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
.
To each such loop
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}
, one can associate a quantity
\varphi(\ell)
equal to
2\pi
times the signed number of turns of
\ell
around the origin — in other words,
\varphi(\ell)
is the
topological winding
of
\ell
around the origin.
Denote by
\mathbb{P}_{\bm{x},\bm{v}}^{\delta}
the conditional probability measure of
\mathcal{T}^{\delta}
given the event
E^{\delta}_{\bm{x},\bm{v}}
defined in (
1.4
).

In Section
2
, we will also show that as
\delta\to 0
, the restriction of the discrete loop measure to
\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}
converges to the analogous quantity for a
Brownian loop measure

\mu^{\textnormal{ND}}_{\Omega}
on
\Omega
, with Neumann boundary condition on
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega
and Dirichlet boundary condition on
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega

(see Section
2.1
and
[
LSW03
,
LW04
]
for definitions).
This does not directly follow from known results such as
[
LF04
]
owing to the presence of the reflecting boundary.
Denote by
\mathcal{L}_{*}
the set of (continuous) loops on
\Omega
and for each such loop
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{*}
, define
\varphi(\ell)
similarly as before.
Using this result combined with an exact asymptotic computation of determinants involving random walk excursion kernels,
we are able to deduce the following result on the law of the total winding of curves.

Recall from the scaling limit Setup (Item
1
) that we denote by
\phi
a fixed conformal map from
\Omega
onto the annulus
\mathbb{A}_{r}
. Choosing the branch of the argument function
\arg
to take values in
[-\pi,\pi)
, set

\displaystyle c:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\arg\phi(x_{j})-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\arg\phi(e_{j}).

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
x_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,x_{n}^{\delta}\in\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega^{\delta}
and
v_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,v_{n}^{\delta}\in\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
be distinct vertices labelled in counterclockwise order along the respective boundaries.
Assume that as
\delta\to 0
, each
x^{\delta}_{j}
converges to
x_{j}\in\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega
and each
v^{\delta}_{j}
converges to
v_{j}\in\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega
,
where
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega
and
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega
are distinct and in counterclockwise order.
For any odd
n\geq 1
, we have the following two cases.

1.

If
\beta\in[0,\frac{1}{2})\cup(\frac{1}{2},1)
,
and
N_{\beta}\in\mathbb{Z}
is such that
|\beta-N_{\beta}|=\underset{k\in\mathbb{Z}}{\min}\,|\beta-k|
, then we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{x},\bm{v}}^{\delta}\Big[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\beta\sum_{j=1}^{n}2\pi K(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})}\Big]=\big(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(\beta-N_{\beta})c}+o_{r}(1)\big)\;r^{|\beta-N_{\beta}|}\;\exp\Big(\mu_{\Omega}^{\textnormal{ND}}\big[(1-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\beta\varphi(\ell)})\,\mathbb{1}{\{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{*}\}}\big]\Big).

(1.5)

2.

If
\beta=\frac{1}{2}
, then we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{x},\bm{v}}^{\delta}\Big[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\beta\sum_{j=1}^{n}2\pi K(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})}\Big]=\big(2\cos(c/2)+o_{r}(1)\big)\;r^{{1}/{2}}\;\exp\Big(\mu_{\Omega}^{\textnormal{ND}}\big[(1-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\beta\varphi(\ell)})\,\mathbb{1}{\{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{*}\}}\big]\Big).

(1.6)

For any even
n\geq 2
, denoting
N_{\beta}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
|\beta-N_{\beta}-\frac{1}{2}|=\underset{k\in\mathbb{Z}}{\min}\,|\beta-k-\frac{1}{2}|
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{x},\bm{v}}^{\delta}\Big[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\beta\sum_{j=1}^{n}2\pi K(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})}\Big]=\big(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(\beta-N_{\beta}+\frac{1}{2})c}+o_{r}(1)\big)\;\exp\Big(\mu_{\Omega}^{\textnormal{ND}}\big[\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\varphi(\ell)}(1-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\beta\varphi(\ell)})\,\mathbb{1}{\{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{*}\}}\big]\Big).

(1.7)

The term
o_{r}(1)
is uniform over the locations of
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega
and
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega
.

A remarkable aspect Theorem
1.1
is that, setting aside the dependence of the winding on the initial and end position of the
n
curves (captured by the term
c
),
the behaviour of the right-hand side as the inner boundary shrinks, meaning that
r\to 0
, depends on
n

only through its parity
.
The difference between the odd and even cases comes from certain determinantal identities (whose source can be traced to Fomin’s formula for random walks
[
Fom01
]
, although the edge weights are complex numbers instead of being positive in our case), and a careful analysis of the signs of the permutations which may occur in the expansion of the determinant.
The proof of Theorem
1.1
comprises most of Section
2
.

In the limit as
\delta\to 0
and then
r\to 0
(in this order),
a more precise rigorous derivation of the asymptotic variance of the total winding
K(\bm{\gamma}^{\delta})
as
claimed in (
1.2
) follows from the identification of the scaling limit of the UST branches
\gamma_{j}^{\delta}
as
\delta\to 0
, which we now proceed to describe.

1.3
Convergence to random matrix statistics

We now consider a collection
\bm{\eta}=(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n})
of
n
random curves in the unit disc
\mathbb{D}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\,|\,|z|<1\}
starting at some boundary points on the unit circle
\mathbb{S}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\,|\,|z|=1\}
.
As we will later see, these curves will correspond to the scaling limit of
\bm{\gamma}^{\delta}=(\gamma^{\delta}_{1},\ldots,\gamma^{\delta}_{n})
(conditioned on the event
E^{\delta}_{\bm{x}}
).
First, let
\mathbb{T}^{n}=(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{n}
be the
n
-torus with periodic boundary conditions,
and let
\mathbb{T}_{0}^{n}
denote the subset of elements admitting representatives

\bm{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n})
satisfying
\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}<\cdots<\theta_{n}<\theta_{1}+2\pi
.
Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{n}:=\big\{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{\theta}}=(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{1}},\ldots,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{n}})\in\mathbb{S}^{n}\,|\,\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{T}_{0}^{n}\big\}.

Let the density of the starting points of the curves
\bm{\eta}
on
\mathbb{S}^{n}
be given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\rho(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{\theta}}):=\;&\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{n}}\mathbb{1}{\{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{\theta}}\in\mathcal{X}_{n}\}}\prod_{1\leq j<k\leq n}\big|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{j}}-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{k}}\big|\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{d}\theta_{j},\\
\textnormal{where}\quad\mathcal{Z}_{n}:=\;&\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n}}\mathbb{1}{\{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{\theta}}\in\mathcal{X}_{n}\}}\prod_{1\leq j<k\leq n}\big|\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{j}}-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{k}}\big|\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{d}\theta_{j}.\end{split}

(1.8)

Note that (
1.8
) coincides with the eigenvalue distribution of an
(n\times n)
-random matrix from the classical
Circular Orthogonal Ensemble
(COE, also known as C
\beta
E for
\beta=1
)
[
Dys62b
,
Dys62a
,
For10
]
.

Given these starting points, let the conditional law of
\bm{\eta}
be that of
n
-sided radial
\SLE_{2}
started from
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{\theta}}
.
In other words, given
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{\theta}}
, the curves
\bm{\eta}
can be generated by a radial Loewner evolution
driven by
Dyson Brownian motion
associated to the Circular
\beta
-Ensemble (C
\beta
E)
with parameter

\beta=4
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\Theta_{i}(t)=\sqrt{2}\,\mathrm{d}W_{i}(t)+2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}\cot\bigg(\frac{\Theta_{i}(t)-\Theta_{j}(t)}{2}\bigg)\mathrm{d}t,\qquad 1\leq i\leq n,

(1.9)

where
(W_{i}(t)\colon t\geq 0)
for
1\leq i\leq n
are
n
independent Brownian motions on
\mathbb{R}
with
W_{i}(0)=\theta_{i}
for each
i
.

As mentioned,
\bm{\eta}
is also known as
n
-sided radial SLE (see Section
3.2
).
The relationship of Dyson Brownian motion and SLE theory was first observed by John Cardy
[
Car03
]
,
who used conformal field theory techniques to show how radial
\SLE_{\kappa}
curves can be generated from Dyson Brownian motion with a judicious choice of the parameter
\beta
(namely
\beta=8/\kappa
).
Later,
n
-sided radial SLE was rigorously constructed in
[
HL21
]
.
The key difficulty is that the configurational measure approach to build SLE variants by tilting fails due to the divergence of the Brownian loop measure term at the common endpoint of the curves;
which could also be observed from our formulas in Theorem
1.1
.
(See also
[
AHP24
]
for a large deviations principle as
\kappa\to 0
,
and references for connections to integrable systems.)

There are several natural ways to parametrise the curves
\bm{\eta}
, corresponding to the speed at
which the different curves are discovered (e.g., one can discover all the curves at once, or one at a time).
The above description corresponds to what is called the
common parametrisation

[
HL21
]
,
where the capacity (i.e., the negative log-conformal radius) seen from the origin in the multi-slit disc
4
4

4

Here and throughout, abusing notation and with no danger of confusion, we identify the curves
(\eta_{1}[0,t],\ldots,\eta_{n}[0,t])
with the closed hull
\bm{\eta}[0,t]:=\cup_{j}\eta_{j}[0,t]
of
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
that they generate.

\mathbb{D}\setminus\bm{\eta}[0,t]
equals
nt
.
In this vein, for
1\leq i\leq n
, let
\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\gamma$}}$}{}_{i}^{\delta}
denote the time-reversal of the branch
\vec{\gamma}_{i}^{\delta}
, viewed as a (linearly interpolated and hence continuous) curve from
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
to
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega^{\delta}
.
We reparametrise
(\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\gamma$}}$}{}_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\gamma$}}$}{}_{n}^{\delta})
to obtain curves
\bm{\eta}^{\delta}=(\eta_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,\eta_{n}^{\delta})
such that the common parametrisation condition holds:

\displaystyle-\log\CR(0;\mathbb{D}\setminus\bm{\eta}^{\delta}[0,t])=nt.

To state our scaling limit result, which roughly speaking says that the discrete branches
\bm{\eta}^{\delta}
converge to the above SLE variant, let us describe in more detail the framework we use for this convergence.
Let
(\Omega_{r})_{0\leq r\leq 1}
denote a family of doubly connected domains as in the above Setup (Item
1
).
For the sake of explaining the idea, we will assume (although that is not really necessary)
that
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega_{r}
does not depend on
r
, and
\textnormal{diam}(\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega_{r})\to 0
as
r\to 0
,
so in fact
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega_{r}
converges with respect to the Hausdorff distance to
0\in\Omega_{\scalebox{0.6}{{fill}}}
.
In this way, the conformal isomorphism
\phi_{r}\colon\Omega_{r}\to\mathbb{A}_{r}
can simply be taken to be the restriction to
\Omega_{r}
of the fixed conformal isomorphism

\phi\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{D}
with
\phi(0)=0
and
\phi^{\prime}(0)>0
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Conditionally on the event
E^{\delta}_{\bm{x}}
defined in (
1.1
), we have

\displaystyle(\phi(\eta_{1}^{\delta}),\ldots,\phi(\eta_{n}^{\delta}))\longrightarrow(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n})

in law, uniformly on compact intervals of time as
\delta\to 0
and then
r\to 0
.

This result establishes the convergence of the UST branches on the event
E^{\delta}_{\bm{x}}

to a multiple radial Loewner evolution driven by the circular Dyson Brownian motion with
\beta=4
as in (
1.9
),
thus verifying Cardy’s prediction
[
Car03
]
.
The general relation
\beta=8/\kappa
also explains why we should not be surprised to obtain C
\beta
E with
\beta=1
in the hitting distribution (
1.8
) — indeed, in that case the relevant value of
\kappa
is that of the associated UST Peano curves, meaning
\kappa=8

[
Sch00
,
LSW04
]
.

An interesting feature of Theorem
1.2
is that
in particular, the
n
positions where the curves
\bm{\eta}^{\delta}
hit the outer boundary
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
of the domain
converge in law as
\delta\to 0
and then
r\to 0
to the COE distribution obtained as a pullback of (
1.8
) on the configuration space
\mathcal{X}_{n}
by
\phi
.
This particular fact was conjectured by Arista and O’Connell
[
AO19
]
and already proved by Kenyon in the cases
n=2,3
in
[
Ken00b
]
.

One might suspect that a first step to describe the geometry of the branches
\bm{\eta}^{\delta}
will be to obtain sharp asymptotics on the partition function
\mathbb{P}^{\delta}[E^{\delta}_{\bm{x}}]
.
This is only partly true, in that we only need to determine the asymptotics of the partition function when
n
is odd. (In fact, in the proof of Proposition
3.1
in Section
3.1
we obtain a much more precise result, including the determination of the leading constant.)

Theorem 1.3
.

For the event
E^{\delta}_{\bm{x}}
defined in (
1.1
) with
\bm{x}^{\delta}=(x_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,x_{n}^{\delta})
on the inner boundary
and the domain
\Omega_{r}
conformally equivalent to the annulus
\mathbb{A}_{r}
,
when
n\geq 3
is odd, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\delta}[E^{\delta}_{\bm{x}}]=r^{\frac{n^{2}-1}{4}\,+\,o(1)},\qquad\textnormal{where $o(1)\to 0$ as $\delta\to 0$ followed by $r\to 0$.}

Theorem
1.2
also gives us access to the winding of the curves
\bm{\eta}

in the common parametrisation.
This appears, in fact, relatively easy thanks to the explicit form of the SDE system (
1.9
) and the close connection between topological winding and Loewner driving function.
Let
\varphi_{i}(t):=\arg\eta_{i}(t)-\arg\eta_{i}(0)
denote the
topological winding
around the origin of
\eta_{i}[0,t]

(which measures the change in the argument in some branch choice; see
[
BLR20
]
for the relation between topological and intrinsic winding).
The next result is a multi-curve version of Schramm’s result
[
Sch00
, Theorem 7.2]
.

Proposition 1.4
.

Let
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. The following convergence holds in law as
t\to\infty
:

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\varphi_{1}(t)}{\sqrt{2t/n}},\ldots,\frac{\varphi_{n}(t)}{\sqrt{2t/n}}\Big)\longrightarrow(X,\ldots,X).

Taking into account the time-parametrisation, note that
Proposition
1.4
can be viewed as a rigorous version of the winding variance (
1.2
).
The result is stated for convenience in the case of
\kappa=2
, which is of interest to us in the context of the uniform spanning tree.
However, we have written the proof in fact for general
n
-sided radial
\SLE_{\kappa}
in Section
3.3
:
in this case, the same result holds, but the term
\sqrt{2/n}
should be replaced by
\sqrt{\kappa/n}
,
yielding the variance
\sim\kappa/n^{2}
as predicted in
[
WW03
]
.

1.4
Flow-line coupling for
n
-sided radial SLE with GFF

To the pair of uniform spanning trees defined by
\mathcal{T}^{\delta}
and its dual one can associate, via a form of Temperley’s bijection
[
KPW00
,
Dub09
,
BLR16
]
,
a dimer configuration living on the medial graph (Figure
1.2
).
The dimer model has a natural height function, which converges to the Gaussian free field (GFF)
[
Ken00a
,
Ken01
]
.
As we will not rely on the dimer interpretation,
we do not give precise definitions here (which could, however, be inferred from Figure
1.2
; see also
[
BLR25
]
for a closely related combinatorial setup and bijection, and
[
BLR24
]
for a scaling limit result).

Given the close relations between dimers and imaginary geometry, it is natural to wonder whether the
n
-sided curves
\bm{\eta}=(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n})
can be coupled with a GFF-like object as “flow lines”.
In the result below, we derive such a coupling (not only for
\kappa=2
but for arbitrary
\kappa\in(0,4)
).
The theory of flow lines for the GFF was extensively developed by Miller and Sheffield
[
MS16
,
MS17
]
, who coined the term “imaginary geometry”,
and earlier by Dubédat
[
Dub09
]
who focused on the role of partition functions.
The series of works
[
BLR20
,
BLR24
,
BLR25
]

addresses the case of present interest.

Figure 1.2
:
The dimer configuration associated to
\mathcal{T}^{\delta}
and its dual.
The underlying tree and color coding are identical to those in Figure
1.1
.
In the right panel, we show the associated height function. The conventions for the height are indicated at the top and right of this picture (black vertices are either crosses or black dots; all other vertices are white).
By convention, the top right corner has height zero and the height increases by
\pm 1
across edges without dimers (and by
\pm 3
otherwise). The height function is only well defined after introducing a branch cut
\zeta
(in red), which entails an additional jump of
n+1
(times 4 with this normalisation, so
+12
in this example).

Theorem 1.5
.

Fix
\kappa\in(0,4)
and
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{\theta}}\in\mathcal{X}_{n}
.
Let
\chi_{\kappa}:=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\kappa}}-\frac{\sqrt{\kappa}}{2}
and
\lambda_{\kappa}:=\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{\kappa}}
.
Consider the harmonic function
\,\mathfrak{h}\colon\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{R}
defined for
w\in\mathbb{D}
as

\displaystyle\,\mathfrak{h}(w)=\,\mathfrak{h}_{\bm{\theta}}(w):=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\arg\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{j}}-w}{1-\overline{w}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{j}}}\bigg)=-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\arg(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{j}}-w)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\theta_{j}.

(1.10)

Let
\mathrm{h}
be a Gaussian free field with Dirichlet boundary conditions in
\mathbb{D}
,
and
\bm{\vartheta}=(\vartheta_{1},\ldots,\vartheta_{n})
flow lines of

\displaystyle\Phi(\cdot):=\mathrm{h}(\cdot)+\Big(\chi_{\kappa}+\frac{n}{\sqrt{\kappa}}\Big)\arg(\cdot)+\,\mathfrak{h}_{\bm{\theta}}(\cdot)

(1.11)

starting from
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\bm{\theta}}
and with respective angles
\alpha_{j}=\frac{2\lambda_{\kappa}(j-1)}{\chi_{\kappa}}
for
1\leq j\leq n
.
After parameterising the curves by their common parametrisation, the law of
\bm{\vartheta}

agrees with that of the
n
-sided radial
\SLE_{\kappa}
curves
\bm{\eta}
.

In other words, there exists a coupling between
\Phi
and
\bm{\eta}

such that for all stopping times
\tau
,
conditionally given
\bm{\eta}[0,\tau]=(\eta_{1}[0,\tau],\ldots,\eta_{n}[0,\tau])
, we have

\displaystyle\Phi|_{\mathbb{D}_{\tau}}=\tilde{\Phi}\circ g_{\tau}-\chi_{\kappa}\arg g^{\prime}_{\tau},

(1.12)

where
\tilde{\Phi}
has the same law as (
1.11
), and
g_{\tau}\colon\mathbb{D}_{\tau}\to\mathbb{D}
is the unique conformal isomorphism uniformising the complement of
\mathbb{D}_{\tau}:=\mathbb{D}\setminus\bm{\eta}[0,\tau]

normalised such that
g_{\tau}(0)=0
and
g^{\prime}_{\tau}(0)>0
.

Remark 1.6
.

We now make a few remarks about the theorem above.

•

In common with the literature on imaginary geometry
[
MS16
,
MS17
]

and with
[
BLR20
]
,
our convention here is to normalise
\mathrm{h}
so that
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{h}(z)\mathrm{h}(w)]=-\log|z-w|+O(1)
as
|z-w|\to 0
.

•

In Equation (
1.10
), the function
\arg(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{j}}-\cdot)
(and thus the function
\,\mathfrak{h}
)
is a well-defined (single-valued) function on
\mathbb{D}
,
since we can use a branch cut for
\arg(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta_{j}}-\cdot)
which remains outside of
\mathbb{D}
.

•

However,
z\mapsto\arg z
cannot be defined as a single-valued function on
\mathbb{D}
.
We may choose, for instance,
\arg z\in[\pi/2,5\pi/2)
with discontinuity in the positive imaginary axis; note the analogy with Figure
1.2
.

•

Theorem
1.5
says that the curves
\bm{\eta}
are flow lines of
\mathrm{h}+(n+1)\chi_{\kappa}\arg+\,\mathfrak{h}
.
Using the methods in
[
BLR20
]
,
one can in fact show as a corollary of Theorem
1.5
that the limiting height function
(with respect to the winding flow)
of the corresponding dimer model is
\mathrm{h}/\chi_{\kappa}+(n+1)\arg+\,\mathfrak{h}/\chi_{\kappa}
.

1.5
A paradox and its resolution

One of the most interesting aspects of Theorem
1.5
is that the field
\mathrm{h}
in (
1.11
) is just a standard Gaussian free field: it does not have any unusual singularity at the origin
(even though the Loewner evolution of the curves themselves is highly singular near the origin).
In particular, if
\mathrm{h}_{\epsilon}
denotes the circle average of
\mathrm{h}
at distance
\epsilon>0
, then
independently
of
n
, its variance reads

\displaystyle\textnormal{var}((1/\chi_{\kappa})\mathrm{h}_{\epsilon}(0))=2\log(1/\epsilon).

However, in Temperley’s bijection, the winding of curves corresponds to the height function, so the fact that the leading-order coefficient in this variance is independent of
n
appears to be in contradiction with Proposition
1.4
. Indeed, both Proposition
1.4
and its more precise version, Theorem
1.1
, indicate that the winding of curves has a variance of order
(2/n^{2})\log(1/\epsilon)
, rather than
2\log(1/\epsilon)
. In other words, in both Proposition
1.4
and Theorem
1.1
, the law of the winding of each curve is affected by the number of curves — which makes sense, because of the rigidity discussed above (
1.2
) (the more curves, the more rigid the system — hence, the less the winding of each curve).
But the variance of the associated
height function
near the origin does not depend on
n
,
even though (via Temperley’s bijection) it is supposed to measure the winding of the curves!
The resolution of this apparent paradox will be explained below.

Remark 1.7
.

As we were preparing to write our results, we learnt from Rick Kenyon that he had already considered in his work
[
Ken00b
]
the problem of conditioning a uniform spanning tree on the event
E^{\delta}_{\bm{x}}
of consideration here, at least in the cases
n=2
and
n=3
.
In
[
Ken00b
, Top of page 2]
, he writes:

“A final computation is the asymptotic “winding number” of the branches of the tree. For the UST on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, there is almost surely a unique branch from the origin to
\infty
.
In the annulus of inner
radius
r
and outer radius
R
concentric about the origin, we show that the total turning of this
branch about the origin has variance tending to
(1/2)\log(R/r)+O(1)
, as
R/r\to\infty
while
r\to\infty
.
Surprisingly, this same variance holds even if the origin is conditioned on having two or three
branches to
\infty
.”

The factor “
1/2
” above is probably a typo — as we indicated above, the winding when there is no conditioning should be given by
2\log(1/\epsilon)
, instead of
(1/2)\log(1/\epsilon)
.
More intriguingly, the setting described in the above paragraph is in particular covered by our Theorem
1.1
, so that
our result contradicts the conclusion in

[
Ken00b
, Theorem 6.1]
.
Namely, according to Theorem
1.1
, the variance should be divided by
4
and
9
, respectively, instead of remaining the same.
The error in the argument of
[
Ken00b
]

appears to us to be similar to the paradox mentioned in the previous paragraph: indeed, Kenyon first computes the asymptotic dimer-dimer correlation, and then deduces asymptotics for the corresponding height function.
In particular, he then deduces that the variance of the height function at the inner boundary does not depend on
n
, from which his conclusion seemingly follows.

Resolution.

Our resolution of the paradox comes from the fact that the dimers live on a non-simply connected graph, and the height function
\mathrm{h}^{\delta}
is only well defined as a function (so we can speak about its variance, say)

if we take into account the jump across the branch cut

(see again Figure
1.2
).

First, let us briefly recall the relation between winding and height function in the simply connected (say, Temperleyan) case. Consider a
path going from one face to another, staying parallel — immediately adjacent — to a branch from the associated uniform spanning tree. Such a path does not encounter any dimer, as per definition of Temperley’s bijection.
Hence the height function, as we travel along this path, oscillates between two values differing by
\pm 1
, on each straight portion of the path, and has an extra jump at each corner. Overall, the height difference accumulated along this path between faces with the right parity is thus equal to the winding of the path (see
[
KPW00
,
BLR16
,
BLR25
]
).

Now, consider what happens in the non simply connected case.
Then, in addition to the winding of the path, one also has to take into account all the times when the path jumps across the branch cut. Thus, each time it crosses the branch cut, the path also accumulates a value given by

\mp(n+1)2\pi
(if the height is measured with respect to the winding reference flow, i.e., in units of
2\pi

and the system has
n
radial branches as in Figure
1.2
).
Thus overall, as we go from inside to outside, if the number of turns across the annulus is
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
then the height difference will be equal to (up to unimportant
O(1)
errors):

\displaystyle\mathrm{h}^{\delta}(f^{\prime})-\mathrm{h}^{\delta}(f)=2k\pi-k(n+1)2\pi=-2nk\pi=-n\big(2\pi K(\gamma_{1}^{\delta})\big),

at two squares
f,f^{\prime}
.
As the winding has a variance proportional to
2/n^{2}
, it is no wonder that the height difference should have a variance independent of
n
!

Remark 1.8
.

In fact, in the the physics literature, Wieland and Wilson
[
WW03
]

had investigated numerically the variance of the winding of various random fractal curves, including loop-erased random walks,
and including the setup of the present article.
In particular, they expected (and numerically verified) that when
n
strands whose scaling limit is expected to be an
\SLE_{\kappa}
curve meet at an interior point, the variance of the winding angle scales as
(\kappa/n^{2})\log(R/r)
.
Our work confirms these numerical results; see the comments after Proposition
1.4
.
This is also consistent with the winding spectrum conjectured by Duplantier and Binder using Coulomb gas methods
5
5

5

In the Coulomb gas formalism of conformal field theory, for each
\kappa\in(0,8]
, the common interior endpoint of the
n
branches should correspond to a primary field of conformal weight
2h_{0,n/2}(\kappa)=\frac{4n^{2}-(\kappa-4)^{2}}{8\kappa}
(equalling
\frac{n^{2}-1}{4}
when
\kappa=2
). Interestingly enough, the conformal weight
2h_{0,1/2}(\kappa)=\frac{(6-\kappa)(\kappa-2)}{8\kappa}
for
n=1
vanishes at
\kappa=2
. See also Lemma
3.5
.

(see
[
Dup06
, Section 10.6]

and
[
DB02
,
HPW25
]
).

2
Winding identities: proof of Theorem
1.1

In this section, we will derive the exact winding identity stated in Theorem
1.1
.
Some formulas obtained in this section will also be used in the proof of the convergence of the discrete branches to
\SLE_{2}
(Theorem
1.2
).
We use the same setup and notation as in Section
1.1
.
The first Section
2.1
collects well-known results:
a determinantal formula of Fomin
[
Fom01
]
for random walks,
the algorithm due to Pemantle and Wilson
[
Pem91
,
Wil96
]

relating UST branches with loop-erased random walks,
and definitions for random walk loop soups
[
LF04
]
.
In Section
2.2
, using Fomin’s theorem we express the total winding of the branches in terms of the random walk and its loop soup
(Proposition
2.3
).
Section
2.3
concerns the scaling limit to Brownian loop soup,
and after several key lemmas, we finish the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

2.1
Complex weights, Fomin’s formula, and Random walk loop soup

Fix a simple path on
\zeta
the dual graph of
\Omega^{\delta}=(\mathcal{V}^{\delta},\mathcal{E}^{\delta})
oriented from the inner boundary to the outer boundary
(this is the “
branch cut
” or “
zipper
”, depicted in red in Figure
1.2
).
For each edge
e\in\mathcal{E}^{\delta}
crossing
\zeta
, we distinguish two possible directions:
we let
\vec{\mathcal{E}}{}^{\delta}_{\zeta}
(resp.
\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\mathcal{E}$}}$}{}{}^{\delta}_{\zeta}
)
denote the set of oriented edges crossing
\zeta
from the left side of
\zeta
to the right side of
\zeta

(resp. from the right side to left side).

For a discrete path
P=(y_{0},\ldots,y_{l})
on
\Omega^{\delta}
, let
w_{0}(P)=\big(\prod_{i=0}^{l-1}\deg(y_{i})\big)^{-1}

denote the probability that a simple random walk starting from
y_{0}
and run for time
l
is equal to
P
.
Fix
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, and define

\displaystyle w_{\beta}(P):=w_{0}(P)\,\bigg(\prod_{j=0}^{l-1}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\beta}\,\mathbb{1}{\{(y_{j},y_{j+1})\in\vec{\mathcal{E}}{}^{\delta}_{\zeta}\}}\bigg)\bigg(\prod_{j=0}^{l-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\beta}\,\mathbb{1}{\{(y_{j},y_{j+1})\in\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\mathcal{E}$}}$}{}{}^{\delta}_{\zeta}\}}\bigg).

(2.1)

In words, under
w_{\beta}
, each path
P
receives not only its random walk weight, but also an extra factor
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\beta}
each time it crosses
\zeta_{\delta}
from left to right, and a factor
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\beta}
for each crossing in the opposite direction.

We will recall Fomin’s determinant formula in this setup.
It is more well known in the case of positive weights, but it is equally valid for complex weights as it is simply a consequence of planar topology.
Let
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\mathcal{V}^{\delta}
(resp
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\mathcal{V}^{\delta}
) denote the set of vertices of
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
(resp
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega^{\delta}
).
We define the “
hitting probability
”

\displaystyle h^{\delta}_{\beta}(x,v):=\sum_{P\colon x\to v}w_{\beta}(P),

(2.2)

where the sum is taken over all paths starting from the vertex
x\in\mathcal{V}^{\delta}
and ending at
v\in\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\mathcal{V}^{\delta}
.
Note that if
\beta=0
, then
h^{\delta}_{\beta}(x,v)
really corresponds to the probability that a random walk starting from
x
first touches the outer boundary
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
at
v
.
For
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, the function
h^{\delta}_{\beta}(\cdot,v)
also has a natural interpretation as a discrete harmonic function on the
universal cover
of
\Omega^{\delta}
.
Namely, let
\{\Omega^{\delta}_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}=\{(\mathcal{V}^{\delta}_{m},\mathcal{E}^{\delta}_{m})\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
be copies of
\Omega^{\delta}=\Omega^{\delta}_{0}
,
and denote by
\Omega_{\scalebox{0.6}{{univ}}}^{\delta}
the graph obtained by gluing
\Omega^{\delta}_{m}
and
\Omega^{\delta}_{m+1}
along
\zeta
, for every
m
.
In particular, there are bijections
\mathcal{V}^{\delta}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\delta}_{m}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\delta}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{E}^{\delta}_{m}
given by
v\leftrightarrow v_{m}
and
e\leftrightarrow e_{m}
.
Then,
h^{\delta}_{\beta}(\cdot,v)
can be extended to all of
\Omega_{\scalebox{0.6}{{univ}}}^{\delta}
and is then a discrete harmonic function there,
equalling
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}m\beta}
at
v_{m}
, for every
m
.
In particular, if
\beta=0
, then
h^{\delta}_{\beta}(\cdot,v)
yields back the above probability.
If
\beta=\pi
, then
h^{\delta}_{\beta}(\cdot,v)
also has a natural interpretation in terms a discrete harmonic function defined on a double cover of
\Omega^{\delta}
.

For a path
P
, we let
\mathrm{LE}(P)
denote the
chronological loop-erasure
of
P
.
Performing this operation to a random walk sample with reflecting boundary condition on the inner boundary
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\mathcal{V}^{\delta}
and absorbing boundary condition on the outer boundary
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\mathcal{V}^{\delta}
yields a sample of the loop-erased random walk (LERW).

Theorem 2.1

(Wilson’s algorithm
[
Wil96
]
)
.

Let
y_{0},\ldots,y_{N}
be any enumeration of the set of vertices
\mathcal{V}^{\delta}
.
Define
\mathcal{G}_{0}
as the subgraph consisting of
only the vertex
y_{0}
, and recursively
for
k=1,\ldots,N
define
\mathcal{G}_{k}
as the union of
\mathcal{G}_{k-1}
and a LERW from
y_{k}
to
\mathcal{G}_{k-1}
, independently for each
k
.
Then, the last step of the algorithm,
\mathcal{G}_{N}=\mathcal{T}^{\delta}
,
gives a sample of a UST on
\Omega^{\delta}=(\mathcal{V}^{\delta},\mathcal{E}^{\delta})
.

Equation (
2.2
) applies to random walks starting from
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega^{\delta}
exiting at the outer boundary
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
.
Via Wilsons algorithm, the event
E^{\delta}_{\bm{x},\bm{v}}
defined in (
1.4
) concerns exactly this kinds of UST branches.
The following determinantal formula is our key tool to calculate the total winding of these branches.

Theorem 2.2

(Fomin’s formula
[
Fom01
]
)
.

Fix distinct vertices
v_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,v_{n}^{\delta}\in\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\mathcal{V}^{\delta}

and distinct vertices
x_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,x_{n}^{\delta}\in\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\mathcal{V}^{\delta}
in counterclockwise order.
For
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\det\big(h^{\delta}_{\beta}(x^{\delta}_{i},v_{j}^{\delta})\big)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\sum_{P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}}w_{\beta}(P_{1})\cdots w_{\beta}(P_{n}),

(2.3)

where the sum is over all choices of paths
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}
on
\Omega^{\delta}
such that

P_{j}\colon x^{\delta}_{j}\to v_{\sigma(j)}^{\delta}
for each
1\leq j\leq n

and
\mathrm{LE}(P_{i})\cap P_{j}=\emptyset
for all
1\leq i<j\leq n
.

Intuitively, the random walk is recovered from its loop-erasure by adding a soup of discrete loops on top of it.
The random walk loop measure
\nu
, introduced in
[
LF04
]
, is supported on discrete rooted loops on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
For each loop
\ell
, we assign the weight
\nu(\ell):=\frac{(1/4)^{|\ell|}}{|\ell|}
,
and we let
\bar{\ell}
denote the equivalence class of
\ell
in the equivalence relation
\ell\sim\ell^{\prime}
if
\ell^{\prime}
can be obtained from
\ell
by rerooting.
We denote by

\displaystyle\Lambda[\bar{\ell}]:=\sum_{\ell\in\bar{\ell}}\nu(\ell)

the measure obtained from
\nu
by forgetting the root.
The
random walk loop soup with intensity

\vartheta>0
is the Poisson point process with intensity measure
\vartheta\Lambda
.
In
[
LF04
]
, the authors construct a coupling between Brownian loop soup and random walk loop soup with the same intensity.
To do so, they first scale the loops so that the are on
\delta\mathbb{Z}^{2}
instead of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and define the
lifetime
of the discrete loop
\ell
to be given by
t_{\ell}:=\frac{\delta^{2}}{2}|\ell|
.
(For future reference, note that we have
t_{\ell}\geq\frac{\delta}{2}\diam(\ell)
,
where
\diam(\ell)
is the diameter of the loop
\ell
with respect to the Euclidean distance.)
The authors of
[
LF04
]
then construct a coupling such that, with large probability,
all the rescaled random walk loops above a certain mesoscopic size correspond uniquely to a unique Brownian loop, and are very well (uniformly) approximated by it.

In the present article, we will consider the random walk loop soup with Neumann boundary condition on
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega^{\delta}
and Dirichlet boundary condition on
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
.
The presence of a Neumann boundary condition introduces a number of technicalities which we briefly explain.
For each loop
\ell=(v_{0},\ldots,v_{|\ell|})
of duration
|\ell|
with root
v_{0}=v_{|\ell|}\in\mathcal{V}^{\delta}\setminus\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\mathcal{V}^{\delta}
, we assign the weight

\displaystyle\nu^{\textnormal{ND}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}(\ell):=\frac{1}{|\ell|}\Big(\prod_{i=0}^{|\ell|}\deg(v_{i})\Big)^{-1}.

We similarly define
\Lambda_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{ND}}
to be the measure obtained from
\nu_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{ND}}
by forgetting the root.
In this case, we still denote by
t_{\ell}:=\frac{\delta^{2}}{2}|\ell|
the lifetime of
\ell
,
and we parametrise
\ell
by setting
\ell(s):=v_{[2s/\delta^{2}]}
for
0\leq s\leq t_{\ell}
.

Note that (with a small abuse of notation), if
F
is a functional on rooted loops that is invariant under rerooting (so that
F
can also be seen as a functional on
unrooted
loops), we have

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\textnormal{ND}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}[F]=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{\bar{\ell}}\Lambda^{\textnormal{ND}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}(\bar{\ell})\,F(\bar{\ell})\;=\;\sum_{\bar{\ell}}\sum_{\ell\in\bar{\ell}}\nu(\ell)\,F(\ell)\;=\;\sum_{\ell}\nu(\ell)F(\ell)

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{z\in\mathcal{V}^{\delta}\setminus\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\mathcal{V}^{\delta}}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\textnormal{ s.t. }|\ell|=m\textnormal{ and }\\
\textnormal{ the root of $\ell$ is }z\end{subarray}}\frac{w_{0}(\ell)}{m}\,F(\ell).

After parameterising the loop by
t\in[0,\infty)
in such a way at time
t
its position equals that of the
[2t/\delta^{2}]
-th step of the original loop,
we obtain

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\textnormal{ND}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}[F]=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{V}^{\delta}\setminus\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\mathcal{V}^{\delta}}\sum_{t\in\frac{1}{2}\delta^{2}\mathbb{N}}\frac{\delta^{2}}{2t}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\textnormal{ s.t. }|\ell|=t\textnormal{ and }\\
\textnormal{ the root of $\ell$ is }z\end{subarray}}w_{0}(\ell)\,F(\ell).

(2.4)

According to the right side of (
2.4
), we will occasionally, with a slight of notation abuse, view
\Lambda^{\textnormal{ND}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}
as a measure on rooted loops.
The
random walk loop soup
with Neumann boundary condition on
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega^{\delta}
and Dirichlet boundary condition on
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
is the Poisson point process with intensity measure
\Lambda^{\textnormal{ND}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}
.

As part of our argument, we will need to prove the convergence of the measure on the left-hand side of (
2.4
) — more precisely,
the convergence of the conditional law
\mathsf{P}^{\delta}[\,\cdot\,|\,\mathcal{R}(0)=\mathcal{R}(t^{\delta})=z^{\delta}]

of the random walk
\mathcal{R}\sim\mathsf{P}^{\delta}
to return to its root at time
t^{\delta}
, to a Brownian bridge (with mixed boundary conditions) of duration
t
;
and the convergence of the rescaled probability
\delta^{-2}\,\mathsf{P}^{\delta}[\mathcal{R}(t^{\delta})=z^{\delta}\,|\,\mathcal{R}(0)=z^{\delta}]
.
This will be done in Proposition
2.6
, with
z^{\delta}\to z\in\Omega
and
t^{\delta}\to t\in(0,\infty)
as
\delta\to 0
.
Denote the limits by

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathsf{P}^{\delta}[\,\cdot\,|\,\mathcal{R}(0)=\mathcal{R}(t^{\delta})=z^{\delta}]=:\;

\displaystyle\mu^{\textnormal{ND}}_{\Omega}(z,z;t)[\,\cdot\,],

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\delta^{-2}\,\mathsf{P}^{\delta}[\mathcal{R}(t^{\delta})=z^{\delta}\,|\,\mathcal{R}(0)=z^{\delta}]=:\;

\displaystyle p^{\textnormal{ND}}_{\Omega}(z,z;t)

(the former as a weak limit of measures). Note that here, we parameterise the random walk
\mathcal{R}
by letting
\mathcal{R}(s)
be the
[2s/\delta^{2}]
:th lattice step, for
s\geq 0
.
We define the corresponding (Brownian) loop measure by

\displaystyle\mu_{\Omega}^{\textnormal{ND}}[\,\cdot\,]:=\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{d}z\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\;\frac{p_{\Omega}^{\textnormal{ND}}(z,z;t)}{t}\,\mu_{\Omega}^{\textnormal{ND}}(z,z;t)[\,\cdot\,].

(2.5)

A consequence of our results
6
6

6

Without the reflecting boundary conditions, this would essentially follow trivially from, e.g.,
[
LF04
]
.

is that the discrete loop measure
\Lambda^{\textnormal{ND}}_{\Omega^{\delta}}
converges as
\delta\to 0
to
\mu_{\Omega}^{\textnormal{ND}}
.
In the present work, the
Brownian loop soup
on
\Omega
with Neumann boundary condition on
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega

and Dirichlet boundary condition on
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega
is the Poisson point process with intensity measure
\mu_{\Omega}^{\textnormal{ND}}
.

Historically, the Brownian loop measure
\mu
introduced in
[
LSW03
,
LW04
]
has become an important tool in probability theory and geometry of random fractals.
It is an infinite, sigma-finite, conformally invariant measure supported on the set of unrooted continuous loops on
\mathbb{C}
.
For a bounded domain
D\subset\mathbb{C}
, denote by
\mu_{D}
the restriction of
\mu
on loops supported on
D
.
Then,
\mu_{D}
is a conformally invariant in the sense that for any conformal isomorphism
f\colon D\to D^{\prime}
, the measure
\mu_{D^{\prime}}
coincides with the pushforward of
\mu_{D}
by
f
.
While the total mass of
\mu_{D}
is infinite (and can be related to the determinant of the Laplacian
[
Jan06
,
Dub09
]
),
for any two disjoint compact sets
V_{1},V_{2}\subset D
, the measure
\mu_{D}(V_{1},V_{2})
of loops in
D
which intersect both
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
is finite (see
[
FL13
]
).
Moreover, the measure satisfies an important restriction covariance property
(see
[
Wer08
, Proposition 3]
for the case useful to our purposes).

2.2
Discrete winding identities: an exact formula

We now use Fomin’s formula (Theorem
2.2
) to derive key exact identities, which allow us to express the total winding of all
the UST branches in terms of the discrete loop soup (Proposition
2.3
).

First, because of planarity, the only permutations
\sigma
which contribute to the right hand side of (
2.3
) are powers of the shift
\sigma=(1\ 2\cdots n)
; i.e., permutations of the form
\sigma^{k}
for
0\leq k\leq n-1
.
Note that the sign is always positive when
n
is odd and it alternates between
\pm 1
when
n
is even.
This will lead to the difference in Theorem
1.1
between these cases, which we will treat separately below.

For every
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, we let
\smash{\Lambda_{\beta}=\Lambda_{\Omega^{\delta},\beta}^{\textnormal{ND}}}
be the signed loop measure, i.e.,
for every discrete loop
\ell
on
\Omega^{\delta}\setminus\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
with length
|\ell|

and with Neumann boundary condition on
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega^{\delta}
and Dirichlet boundary condition on
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
, using (
2.1
) we define its weight to be

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\beta}[\ell]:=\frac{w_{\beta}(\ell)}{|\ell|}.

(2.6)

Denote by
\mathcal{L}^{\delta}
the set of all unrooted loops on
\Omega^{\delta}\setminus\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
.
Let
\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\delta}\subset\mathcal{L}^{\delta}
be the subset of contractible loops, and
\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}=\mathcal{L}^{\delta}\setminus\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\delta}
the subset of noncontractible loops.

Proposition 2.3
.

For any
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, and for any odd
n\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{x},\bm{v}}^{\delta}\Big[\exp\Big(\mathrm{i}\beta\sum_{j=1}^{n}2\pi K(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})\Big)\Big]=

\displaystyle\;\mathrm{e}^{\Lambda_{0}[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}]-\Lambda_{\beta}[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}]}\;\frac{\det\big(h^{\delta}_{2\pi\beta}(x^{\delta}_{i},v_{j}^{\delta})\big)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}}{\det\big(h^{\delta}_{0}(x^{\delta}_{i},v_{j}^{\delta})\big)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}};

(2.7)

and for any even
n\geq 2
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{x},\bm{v}}^{\delta}\Big[\exp\Big(\mathrm{i}\beta\sum_{j=1}^{n}2\pi K(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})\Big)\Big]=

\displaystyle\;\mathrm{e}^{\Lambda_{\pi}[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}]-\Lambda_{2\pi\beta+\pi}[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}]}\;\frac{\det\big(h^{\delta}_{2\pi\beta+\pi}(x^{\delta}_{i},v_{j}^{\delta})\big)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}}{\det\big(h^{\delta}_{\pi}(x^{\delta}_{i},v_{j}^{\delta})\big)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}}.

(2.8)

where
h^{\delta}_{\beta}
is defined in Equation (
2.2
).

The proof of Proposition
2.3
is given in Lemmas
2.4
&
2.5
.
We begin with the case where
n
is odd, which is easier to deal with, since
(essentially as a consequence from Fomin’s formula in Theorem
2.2
)
the partition function can be expressed exactly as a determinant. For a discrete path
\gamma
, let

\displaystyle K(\gamma):=|\gamma\cap\vec{\mathcal{E}}{}^{\delta}_{\zeta}|-|\gamma\cap\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\mathcal{E}$}}$}{}{}^{\delta}_{\zeta}|

(2.9)

be the (algebraic) number of turns by
\gamma
in the annulus.

Lemma 2.4
.

When
n\geq 1
is odd, for the event (
1.4
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\delta}[E^{\delta}_{\bm{x},\bm{e}}]=\det\big(h^{\delta}_{0}(x^{\delta}_{i},v_{j}^{\delta})\big)_{1\leq i,j\leq n},

(2.10)

given by (
2.3
) with
\beta=0
.
Moreover, for any
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
and for any odd
n\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{x},\bm{v}}^{\delta}\Big[\exp\Big(\mathrm{i}\beta\sum_{j=1}^{n}K(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})\Big)\Big]=

\displaystyle\;\mathrm{e}^{\Lambda_{0}[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}]-\Lambda_{\beta}[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}]}\;\frac{\det\big(h^{\delta}_{\beta}(x^{\delta}_{i},v_{j}^{\delta})\big)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}}{\det\big(h^{\delta}_{0}(x^{\delta}_{i},v_{j}^{\delta})\big)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}}.

(2.11)

Proof.

Since
n
is odd, for every
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
which contributes to the right hand side of (
2.3
),
we have
\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)=1
. Taking
\beta=0
in (
2.3
), and invoking Wilson’s algorithm (Theorem
2.1
), we obtain (
2.10
).

It remains to prove (
2.11
).
We decompose a path
P
into its loop-erasure
\Gamma
and the set of loops erased.
As in
[
LL10
, Proposition 9.5.1]
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{P\colon\mathrm{LE}(P)=\Gamma}w_{\beta}(P)=w_{\beta}(\Gamma)\,\exp\Big(\Lambda_{\beta}[\ell\subset\Omega^{\delta}\setminus\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}\;|\;\ell\cap\Gamma\neq\emptyset]\Big).

(2.12)

It will be convenient to introduce the notation
\Lambda_{\beta}[\gamma^{\delta}_{j}\,|\,\gamma^{\delta}_{1},\ldots,\gamma^{\delta}_{j-1}]
to denote the
\Lambda_{\beta}
-mass of loops in
\mathcal{L}^{\delta}
which intersect
\gamma^{\delta}_{j}
but not
\gamma^{\delta}_{1},\ldots,\gamma^{\delta}_{j-1}
.
Note that the weight of each loop in
\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\delta}
under
\Lambda_{\beta}
is actually independent of
\beta
.
Thus, if we fix a permutation
\sigma
, and for each
j
a path
P_{j}
connecting
x^{\delta}_{j}
to
\smash{v_{\sigma(j)}^{\delta}}
,
then letting
\smash{\gamma^{\delta}_{j}=\mathrm{LE}(P_{j})}
, we obtain

\displaystyle w_{\beta}(P_{1})\cdots w_{\beta}(P_{n})=\;

\displaystyle\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{n}w_{\beta}(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})\Big)\exp\Big(\Lambda_{\beta}\big[\gamma^{\delta}_{1}\big]+\cdots+\Lambda_{\beta}\big[\gamma^{\delta}_{n}\,|\,\gamma^{\delta}_{0},\ldots,\gamma^{\delta}_{n-1}\big]\Big).

(2.13)

Consider first the path
P_{1}
and
\gamma^{\delta}_{1}=\mathrm{LE}(P_{1})
.
On the one hand, every loop
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}
is guaranteed to intersect
\gamma^{\delta}_{1}
.
On the other hand, loops which intersect
\gamma^{\delta}_{1}
but are homotopically trivial do not receive any non-trivial complex weights, i.e., have the same weight under both
\Lambda_{0}
and
\Lambda_{\beta}
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\beta}\big[\gamma^{\delta}_{1}\big]=\Lambda_{\beta}\big[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}\big]+\Lambda_{0}\big[\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\delta}\cap\{\ell\textnormal{ such that }\ell\cap\gamma^{\delta}_{1}\neq\emptyset\}\big].

Furthermore, for the remaining loops, observe that
\gamma^{\delta}_{1}
cuts
\Omega^{\delta}
open into a simply connected domain.
Therefore, every loop which intersects
\gamma^{\delta}_{j}
but not
\gamma^{\delta}_{1},\ldots\gamma^{\delta}_{j-1}
(for
j\geq 2
) is necessarily homotopically trivial, and thus receives a weight equal to that under
\Lambda_{0}
.
We deduce that for any
\beta
, we have

\displaystyle\Lambda_{0}\big[\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\delta}(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})\big]=\Lambda_{\beta}\big[\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\delta}(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})\big]:=\;

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\beta}\big[\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\delta}\textnormal{ such that }\ell\cap\gamma_{j}^{\delta}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }\ell\cap\gamma_{k}^{\delta}=\emptyset\textnormal{ for }1\leq k\leq j-1\big]

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\Lambda_{\beta}\big[\gamma^{\delta}_{1}\big]-\Lambda_{\beta}\big[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}\big],&j=1,\\
\Lambda_{0}\big[\gamma^{\delta}_{j}\,|\,\gamma^{\delta}_{1},\ldots,\gamma^{\delta}_{j-1}\big],&j\geq 2.\end{cases}

and thus, combining with (
2.13
) we see that Fomin’s formula (
2.3
) reads

\displaystyle\det\big(h^{\delta}_{\beta}(x^{\delta}_{i},v_{j}^{\delta})\big)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}=\;

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\Lambda_{\beta}[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}]}\sum_{\bm{\gamma}^{\delta}\in E^{\delta}_{\bm{x},\bm{e}}}\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{n}w_{\beta}(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})\Big)\;\mathrm{e}^{\Lambda_{\beta}[\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\delta}(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})]}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\Lambda_{\beta}[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}]}\sum_{\bm{\gamma}^{\delta}\in E^{\delta}_{\bm{x},\bm{e}}}\;\exp\Big(\mathrm{i}\beta\sum_{j=1}^{n}\big(|\gamma_{j}^{\delta}\cap\vec{\mathcal{E}}{}^{\delta}_{\zeta}|-|\gamma_{j}^{\delta}\cap\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\mathcal{E}$}}$}{}{}^{\delta}_{\zeta}|\big)\Big)\;\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{n}w_{0}(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})\Big)\;\mathrm{e}^{\Lambda_{0}[\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\delta}(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})]}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\Lambda_{\beta}[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}]}\sum_{\bm{\gamma}^{\delta}\in E^{\delta}_{\bm{x},\bm{e}}}\;\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}{\beta}\sum_{j=1}^{n}K(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})}\;\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{n}w_{0}(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})\Big)\;\mathrm{e}^{\Lambda_{0}[\mathcal{L}_{0}^{\delta}(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})]}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\Lambda_{\beta}[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}]-\Lambda_{0}[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}]}\;\mathbb{E}^{\delta}\big[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}{\beta}\sum_{j=1}^{n}K(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})}\,\mathbb{1}{\{E^{\delta}_{\bm{x},\bm{e}}\}}\big]

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\Lambda_{\beta}[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}]-\Lambda_{0}[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}]}\;\mathbb{E}_{\bm{x},\bm{v}}^{\delta}\big[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}{\beta}\sum_{j=1}^{n}K(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})}\big]\,\mathbb{P}^{\delta}\big[E^{\delta}_{\bm{x},\bm{e}}\big],

recalling that
\mathbb{E}_{\bm{x},\bm{v}}^{\delta}
is the conditional expectation given
E^{\delta}_{\bm{x},\bm{e}}
.
This proves (
2.11
) due to (
2.10
).
∎

We now turn to the case where
n
is even.
It includes an additional factor with the signed loop measure (
2.6
)
from the alternating signs of the permutations on the right-hand side of Fomin’s formula (
2.3
).

Lemma 2.5
.

When
n\geq 2
is even, for the event (
1.4
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\delta}[E^{\delta}_{\bm{x},\bm{e}}]=\mathrm{e}^{\Lambda_{0}[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}]-\Lambda_{\pi}[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}]}\;\det\big(h^{\delta}_{\pi}(x^{\delta}_{i},v_{j}^{\delta})\big)_{1\leq i,j\leq n},

(2.14)

given by (
2.3
) with
\beta=\pi
.
Moreover, for any
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
and for any even
n\geq 2
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{x},\bm{v}}^{\delta}\Big[\exp\Big(\mathrm{i}\beta\sum_{j=1}^{n}K(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})\Big)\Big]=

\displaystyle\;\mathrm{e}^{\Lambda_{\pi}[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}]-\Lambda_{\beta+\pi}[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}]}\;\frac{\det\big(h^{\delta}_{\beta+\pi}(x^{\delta}_{i},v_{j}^{\delta})\big)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}}{\det\big(h^{\delta}_{\pi}(x^{\delta}_{i},v_{j}^{\delta})\big)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}}.

(2.15)

Proof.

The proof of Equation (
2.14
) is very similar to the proof of Lemma
2.10
, so we only point out the differences.
Due to planarity, for every
(\gamma_{1}^{\delta},\ldots,\gamma_{n}^{\delta})\in E^{\delta}_{\bm{x},\bm{e},k}
realising the event (
1.3
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\big|\gamma_{j}^{\delta}\cap\vec{\mathcal{E}}{}^{\delta}_{\zeta}\big|\;-\;\sum_{j=1}^{n}\big|\gamma_{j}^{\delta}\cap\reflectbox{$\vec{\reflectbox{$\mathcal{E}$}}$}{}{}^{\delta}_{\zeta}\big|=k\mod 2,\qquad\textnormal{for }0\leq k\leq n-1.

(2.16)

After replacing
\beta
by
\beta+\pi
in Fomin’s formula (
2.3
)
and taking into account the alternating signs of the permutations on the right-hand side of (
2.3
),
a similar analysis as in the proof of Lemma
2.10
yields

\displaystyle\det\big(h^{\delta}_{\beta+\pi}(x^{\delta}_{i},v_{j}^{\delta})\big)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}=\;

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\Lambda_{\beta+\pi}[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}]-\Lambda_{\pi}[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}]}\;\mathbb{E}_{\bm{x},\bm{v}}^{\delta}\big[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}{\beta}\sum_{j=1}^{n}K(\gamma_{j}^{\delta})}\big]\,\mathbb{P}^{\delta}\big[E^{\delta}_{\bm{x},\bm{e}}\big],

(2.17)

Equation (
2.15
) follows immediately from (
2.17
), and by taking
\beta=0
in (
2.17
), we obtain (
2.14
).
∎

2.3
Convergence to Brownian loop soup: proof of Theorem
1.1

Both right-hand sides in Proposition
2.3
(corresponding to the case where
n
is odd and even, respectively)
are expressed in terms of a ratio of determinants and an exponential of total mass of loops winding around the origin associated with the complex weights (
2.1
). We will deal with both terms separately, beginning with the loop soup term.
In fact, the latter can simply be re-expressed in terms of the ordinary random walk loop soup (albeit with mixed Neumann and Dirichlet boundary conditions).
For instance, in the odd case (see Equation
2.7
), recalling Campbell’s formula for Poisson point processes, we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\textnormal{RWLS}}^{\delta}\Big[\exp\Big(\mathrm{i}\frac{\beta}{2\pi}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}}\varphi(\ell)\Big)\Big]=\exp\Big(\Lambda_{0}\Big[(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\beta}{2\pi}\varphi(\ell)}-1)\mathbb{1}{\{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}\}}\Big]\Big)=\mathrm{e}^{\Lambda_{\beta}[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}]-\Lambda_{0}[\mathcal{L}_{*}^{\delta}]},

(2.18)

where
\mathbb{E}_{\textnormal{RWLS}}^{\delta}
is the expectation under the random walk loop soup probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{\textnormal{RWLS}}^{\delta}
with Neumann boundary conditions on
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega^{\delta}
and Dirichlet boundary
conditions on
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}
,
and
\varphi(\ell)
is the topological winding of
\ell\in\mathcal{L}^{\delta}
around the origin, equalling
2\pi
times the signed number of turns of
\ell
around
0
.
(When
\beta=0
, the measure
\Lambda_{0}
coincides with the random walk loop measure
\Lambda_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{ND}}
;
and depending on the context it is more natural to write
\Lambda_{0}
or
\Lambda_{\Omega^{\delta}}^{\textnormal{ND}}
, so we keep both notations).

To prove Theorem
1.1
,
our task will thus be to show the convergence of the term in the exponential in the right-hand side to its continuum analogue,
which involves the Brownian loop soup with Neumann boundary conditions on
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega
and Dirichlet boundary
conditions on
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega
(recall that we call
\mu_{\Omega}^{\textnormal{ND}}
the corresponding intensity measure of this Poisson point process). This task will be completed in Lemma
2.10
.
We first need to prove the convergence of
\mathbb{P}_{\textnormal{RWLS}}^{\delta}
as
\delta\to 0
when restricted on set of loops with non-trivial winding.
As mentioned in Section
2.1
, we will prove the convergence of the conditional law
\mathsf{P}^{\delta}[\,\cdot\,|\,\mathcal{R}(0)=\mathcal{R}(t^{\delta})=z^{\delta}]

of the random walk
\mathcal{R}
to return to its root at time
t^{\delta}
to a Brownian bridge of duration
t=\underset{\delta\to 0}{\lim}\,t^{\delta}
;
and the convergence of the rescaled probability
\delta^{-2}\,\mathsf{P}^{\delta}[\mathcal{R}(t^{\delta})=z^{\delta}\,|\,\mathcal{R}(0)=z^{\delta}]
.

The convergence takes place in the set of pairs
(\ell,t_{\ell})
, where
\ell
is a loop in the plane of lifetime
t_{\ell}>0
, endowed with the usual metric up to reparametrisation,

\displaystyle\dist_{\mathcal{L}}\big((\ell,t_{\ell}),(\tilde{\ell},t_{\tilde{\ell}})\big):=|t_{\ell}-t_{\tilde{\ell}}|\,+\,\inf_{\varrho,\tilde{\varrho}}\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\big|\ell(\varrho(s))-\tilde{\ell}(\tilde{\varrho}(s))\big|,

(2.19)

where the infimum is taken over all increasing homeomorphisms
\varrho\colon[0,1]\to[0,t_{\ell}]
and
\varrho\colon[0,1]\to[0,t_{\tilde{\ell}}]
.

Proposition 2.6
.

Suppose
z^{\delta}\to z\in\Omega
and
t^{\delta}\to t\in(0,\infty)
as
\delta\to 0
.
Then as
\delta\to 0
,
the conditional law
\mathsf{P}^{\delta}[\,\cdot\,|\,\mathcal{R}(0)=\mathcal{R}(t^{\delta})=z^{\delta}]
converges weakly
to the Brownian bridge measure
\mu^{\textnormal{ND}}_{\Omega}(z,z;t)
,
and the rescaled probability
\delta^{-2}\,\mathsf{P}^{\delta}[\mathcal{R}(t^{\delta})=z^{\delta}\,|\,\mathcal{R}(0)=z^{\delta}]
converges
to the transition kernel
p^{\textnormal{ND}}_{\Omega}(z,z,t)\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Let
0<c<t
. Fix a bounded and measurable function
f
from
C([0,c],\mathbb{C})
to
\mathbb{R}
.
We choose
\epsilon>0
and
\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}(\epsilon)>0
such that

\dist(z^{\delta},\partial\Omega^{\delta})\geq 2\epsilon
for every
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0})
,
and define recursively sequences of stopping times for the random walk:
we set
T_{1}^{\delta}:=\inf\{s>c\colon\mathcal{R}(s)\in\partial B(z^{\delta},\frac{\epsilon}{2})\}
;
and we set

\displaystyle S^{\delta}_{j}:=\inf\{s>T^{\delta}_{j}\colon\mathcal{R}(s)\in\partial B(z^{\delta},\epsilon)\}\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad T^{\delta}_{j+1}:=\inf\{s>S^{\delta}_{j}\colon\mathcal{R}(s)\in\partial B(z^{\delta},\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})\},\qquad j\geq 1.

By the convergence of discrete harmonic measure, there exists a constant
0<q=q(\epsilon)<1
, such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\delta\in(0,\delta_{0})}\max_{w\in\partial B(z^{\delta},\epsilon)}\,\mathsf{P}^{\delta}_{w}\big[\mathcal{R}\textnormal{ hits }\partial B(z^{\delta},\tfrac{\epsilon}{2})\textnormal{ before hitting }\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega^{\delta}\big]\leq 1-q.

(2.20)

where
\mathsf{P}^{\delta}_{w}
denotes the probability measure for
\mathcal{R}
starting from
w
.
We will use the following observations.

•

Using the strong Markov property, we see that

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}^{\delta}_{z^{\delta}}\big[f(\mathcal{R}[0,c])\,\mathbb{1}{\{\mathcal{R}(t^{\delta})=z^{\delta}\}}\big]

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathsf{E}^{\delta}_{z^{\delta}}\Big[[f(\mathcal{R}[0,c])\,\mathbb{1}{\{T_{j}^{\delta}<\infty\}}\,\mathsf{P}^{\delta}_{\mathcal{R}(T_{j}^{\delta})}\big[\mathcal{R}(t^{\delta}-T_{j}^{\delta})=z^{\delta}\textnormal{ and }\mathcal{R}[0,t^{\delta}-T_{j}^{\delta}]\subset B(z^{\delta},\epsilon)\big]\Big].

•

Denote by
B^{\textnormal{ND}}\sim\mathsf{P}_{z}
the Brownian motion on
\Omega
with reflective boundary
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega
started at
z
and stopped when hitting
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{out}}}\Omega
.
Then, by
[
SV71
]
(since we assumed that the reflective boundary
\partial_{\scalebox{0.6}{{in}}}\Omega
is analytic and thus in particular
C^{2}
— see also
[
BC08
]

and references therein for more recent works on this topic, including an in-depth discussion for the case where the boundary is not necessarily smooth),
we can couple
\mathcal{R}[0,t^{\delta}]
and
B^{\textnormal{ND}}[0,t]
together in such a way that
||\mathcal{R}-B^{\textnormal{ND}}||_{{\infty}}\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
.
In particular, defining the stopping times
\{T_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
for
B^{\textnormal{ND}}
similarly as above,
the law of
(\mathcal{R}[0,c],T_{1}^{\delta},\mathcal{R}(T_{1}^{\delta}),\ldots,T_{N}^{\delta},\mathcal{R}(T_{N}^{\delta}))

converges weakly to the law of
(B^{\textnormal{ND}}[0,c],T_{1},B^{\textnormal{ND}}(T_{1}),\ldots,T_{N},B^{\textnormal{ND}}(T_{N}))
for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

Denote by
\hat{\mathcal{R}}\sim\hat{\mathsf{P}}^{\delta}_{w^{\delta}}
the standard random walk on
\delta\mathbb{Z}^{2}
started at
\hat{\mathcal{R}}(0)=w^{\delta}\in\mathcal{V}^{\delta}
,
and by
\hat{\tau}_{\epsilon}^{\delta}
the hitting time of
\hat{\mathcal{R}}
at
\partial B(z^{\delta},\epsilon)
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}^{\delta}_{w^{\delta}}\big[\mathcal{R}(s^{\delta})=z^{\delta}\textnormal{ and }\mathcal{R}[0,s^{\delta}]\subset B(z^{\delta},\epsilon)\big]

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{P}}^{\delta}_{w^{\delta}}\big[\hat{\mathcal{R}}(s^{\delta})=z^{\delta}\big]\,-\,\hat{\mathsf{P}}^{\delta}_{w^{\delta}}\big[\mathbb{1}{\{\hat{\tau}_{\epsilon}^{\delta}<s^{\delta}\}}\,\hat{\mathsf{P}}^{\delta}_{\hat{\mathcal{R}}(\hat{\tau}_{\epsilon}^{\delta})}\big[\hat{\mathcal{R}}(s^{\delta}-\hat{\tau}_{\epsilon}^{\delta})=z^{\delta}]\big],\qquad s^{\delta}>0.

Similarly, denote by
\hat{B}\sim\hat{\mathsf{P}}_{w}
the standard Brownian motion on
\mathbb{C}
started at
\hat{B}(0)=w\in\Omega
,
and by
\hat{\tau}_{\epsilon}
the hitting time of
\hat{B}
at
\partial B(z,\epsilon)
.
Let
\hat{p}(x,y;u)
be the transition probability of
\hat{B}
from
x
to
y
at time
u
.
Suppose
w^{\delta}\in\partial B(z^{\delta},\frac{\epsilon}{2})
converges to
w\in\partial B(z,\frac{\epsilon}{2})
and
s^{\delta}\to s>0
as
\delta\to 0
.
By the convergence of the law of
\hat{\mathcal{R}}
to the law of
\hat{B}
and the local central limit theorem (e.g.,
[
LL10
, Theorem 2.1.1]
), we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\,\mathsf{P}^{\delta}_{w^{\delta}}\big[\mathcal{R}(s^{\delta})=z^{\delta}\textnormal{ and }\mathcal{R}[0,s^{\delta}]\subset B(z^{\delta},\epsilon)\big]

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\hat{p}(w,z;s)\,-\,\hat{\mathsf{E}}_{w}\big[\mathbb{1}{\{\hat{\tau}_{\epsilon}<s\}}\,\hat{p}(\hat{B}(\hat{\tau}_{\epsilon}),z;s-\hat{\tau}_{\epsilon})\big].

Moreover, there exists a constant
C=C(\epsilon)>0
, such that

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}^{\delta}_{w^{\delta}}\big[\mathcal{R}(s^{\delta})=z^{\delta}\textnormal{ and }\mathcal{R}[0,s^{\delta}]\subset B(z^{\delta},\epsilon)\big]\leq\hat{\mathsf{P}}^{\delta}_{w^{\delta}}\big[\hat{\mathcal{R}}(s^{\delta})=z^{\delta}\big]\leq C\delta^{2}.

(2.21)

•

By (
2.20
) and the strong Markov property, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}^{\delta}_{v}[T_{j}^{\delta}<\infty]\leq(1-q)^{j},\qquad v\in\Omega^{\delta},\;\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}),\;j\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.22)

Combining these four observations, taking the test function
f\equiv 1

we obtain the convergence of the rescaled probability

\delta^{-2}\,\mathsf{P}^{\delta}_{z^{\delta}}[\mathcal{R}(t^{\delta})=z^{\delta}]
, as well as
the convergence under the conditional probability measure
\mathsf{P}^{\delta}_{z^{\delta}}[\,\cdot\,|\,\mathcal{R}(t^{\delta})=z^{\delta}]
of the random walk
\mathcal{R}[0,c]
to the Brownian motion
B^{\textnormal{ND}}[0,c]
for every
0<c<t
.

It remains to prove that for every
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, we can choose
c>0
which is close to
t
such that

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}^{\delta}_{z^{\delta}}\Big[\max_{u\in[c,t^{\delta}]}|\mathcal{R}(u)-z^{\delta}|>\epsilon\;\Big|\;\mathcal{R}(t^{\delta})=z^{\delta}\Big]\leq\epsilon^{\prime},

(2.23)

which is a sort of a continuity estimate. To this end,
note that there exists a constant
